[Title] ChandRush Chapter 03 Manuscript
[Subtitle] The Parade of Wrong Sizes
[MLabel] Integrated manuscript V01 • continuity corrected 27 October 2026
[MMeta] Complete supplied Chapter 03 story and Friendly Teacher Maths Draft 01, with exact reader copy and non-printing instructions for later pagination, panelisation and page production.
[MScene] How to use this manuscript
[MMeta] All scene, beat, teaching-block, figure and companion IDs are non-printing production references. Word pages are review pages, not graphic-novel page assignments. Final pages remain 4:3 landscape; page dimensions, typography, panel divisions and artwork placement belong to the later workflow.
[MMeta] PRINT CAPTION and PRINT SPEECH labels identify exact reader-facing words beneath them. Speaker names, tail targets, source attributions, locations, view descriptions and staging are non-printing. Never print a source attribution as part of a balloon. Preserve the order of captions and dialogue within each beat.
[MMeta] Each visual beat specifies one frozen image. Earlier or later actions explicitly placed between images are continuity context, not a request for multiple bodies or poses in one picture. A source caption may describe several actions across the illustrated sequence. Silent beats show necessary handovers, setup or intermediate gestures without invented dialogue.
[MMeta] The current beat’s explicit state overrides only the corresponding scene default; all other geography, possessions and equipment states persist. Later planners must copy the applicable location, scene and beat context to every receiving page and panel. Resolve named references before generating a panel. Do not copy a future solved state into an earlier question.
[MMeta] Teaching headings, explanations, examples, questions, working, answers, tables, diagrams and caption sentences are reader-facing. Supplied paragraphs styled Production Note remain non-printing. Technical V identifiers are separated from figure captions into metadata. Every question, required visual and answer remains in source order; question blanks stay blank until the solution.
[MMeta] Use exact inscriptions specified inside production notes as well as labelled speech and caption fields. Put values on their named garment tags, plans, records, arch casing or board. Do not add new signs to fill scenery. The source narration may also refer to an inscription; this is intentional, not permission to invent an extra balloon.
[MMeta] The teacher addresses the reader directly. Lessons do not move characters, consume dangerous story time or repair equipment. The reader can pause or return without changing the adventure’s clock. No magical time-freezing device, speed test or extra emergency is added.
[MMeta] Companion insets are independent character-and-scenery moments at completed teaching boundaries. Their scenery may extend into available space after the lesson is laid out, but never behind equations or across a reading path. They do not advance events or reveal a pending story answer.
[MMeta] Keep illustration identity continuous with the approved Chapter 01 and 02 character assets. Do not redesign faces, skin appearance, hair, clothing, ages or proportions while changing poses. The supplied story establishes Tara as elderly. A reference picture of the correctly dressed clown is an illustration on a stand, not a second live clown.
[MScene] Opening and closing continuity
[MMeta] Opening: Food Street works. Ahana carries the brass work lamp in her right hand; her sketchbook stays packed. Tara holds her staff in her right hand; previous evidence is secured in her sash. Navya has no ankle injury. Tez retains the faded stripe patches from Chapter 02; no new restoration occurs. The Bell is still missing.
[MMeta] The first forty guests have been admitted to Entry Court before the chapter opens, as the supplied narration states. They are escorted to Moon Square during this chapter. The next twenty-two include Ira, Kabir and the girl in green with her friends. The trio are volunteers outside the sixty-two guest total. Food-delivery lists do not determine these admission groups.
[MMeta] Veer’s adult face first appears in Scene CH03 06A. He keeps the stolen counting strip after his escape. His ticket, walking stick, strip and later keys are separate objects. His spare-clothes theft and clown disguise occur in later chapters; only the available outfit, hat and stage paint are established here.
[MMeta] Ending: the board still displays 40 despite sixty-two guests. The folding benches have been stopped. No guest is actually missing. The thirty/twelve/twenty distribution is production continuity for Chapter 04, not an added Chapter 03 teaching task. End on Navya’s supplied reassurance; do not solve Chapter 04.
[MScene] Character register
[MBeat] Ahana
[MMeta] Eleven; short loose black curls, teal jacket over burnt-orange shirt, charcoal cargo trousers, blue-and-white trainers, navy backpack. Lamp right on arrival; parked at the costume bench during work; retrieved before leaving for the arch. Measuring tape travels with her from the costume bench to the safe work stand, then rests on the ribbon bench before she catches the trolley. Both hands must be available for that catch.
[MBeat] Vihaan
[MMeta] Eleven; short side-swept black hair, mustard-yellow jacket, navy shirt and trousers, light trainers, red backpack. Invitation secured. He reaches towards a wheel but never lifts it; later reads the already-checked strap length.
[MBeat] Navya
[MMeta] Eleven; two practical black braids, burgundy overshirt over striped ivory top, blue cargo jeans, pale trainers, tan satchel and small tool pouch. She measures and fits cloth, handles the small model, indicates marks from the safe side, then checks the casing and opens the register.
[MBeat] Tara
[MMeta] Elderly, tall and broad-shouldered, long silver-white braid, indigo robe with restrained gold embroidery, ivory inner clothes, teal sash and practical footwear. Staff right by default; visibly supported for two-handed model, strap and writing tasks. No simultaneous staff grip and two-handed work. Staff ornament is not the stolen Bell.
[MBeat] Tez
[MMeta] Orange tiger cub, cream muzzle/chest/paws, blue stripes with existing faded patches, navy scarf and moon clasp. Remains below knee height. His greeting uses one forepaw against his nose; his useful brake-scene action pins cloth, never a heavy trolley or float.
[MBeat] Ira and Kabir
[MMeta] Ira is nine, smaller, short ponytail, pale powder-blue jacket over ivory, charcoal leggings, white trainers and cream cross-body purse. Her saved cup drawing stays in that purse. Kabir is twelve, slightly taller than the trio, short thick black hair, rust hoodie, dark joggers, pale trainers and wristwatch. His notebook and pencil are pocketed when he needs free hands.
[MBeat] Wooden clown
[MMeta] Tall and slender, ivory-painted wooden face, round red nose, blue carved curls and eyebrows, brass wrists, red jacket with blue sleeves, checked trousers, striped oversized shoes, pointed red-and-gold hat. Never speaks. His near overlong sleeve is replaced and retained as the one identifiable comic sleeve. Other loose cloth is tucked and fastened during fitting so both hands are available. The costume box remains on his light red-and-blue trolley beneath a fixed cushion rack. Before moving the trolley, he leaves his brush in the open colour case and the ribbon on its mat at the ribbon bench; both hands are empty.
[MBeat] Veer
[MMeta] Tall, lean, middle-aged human man, angular face, dark eyes and dark hair silvering at the temples. Plum knee-length coat over charcoal clothes, dark trousers and practical boots. Dark walking stick usually right; service ticket left when used. Strip is pocketed before ticket use at the second escape; no impossible three-object grip.
[MBeat] Helpers and Pip
[MMeta] Wooden helpers have honey-coloured grain, painted eyes, brass joints, broad feet and separate wooden thumbs. The broad blue-waistcoated helper escorts the first forty, returns, then carries heavy float parts. One distinct parade helper checks the float, holds its stop and later watches the arch stair. Plain escort and seating helpers are separate individuals. Pip is one palm-sized brass bird with a single spring clip; it carries a folded written warning, not spoken answers.
[MScene] Location masters
[MBeat] Location CH03 L01 Parade Ground master
[MMeta] View from Food Street towards the counting arch: protected pedestrian lane follows the physical-left edge; central rehearsal loop is empty; elephant work bay is on the physical right. Costume bench is outside the loop beside the near part of the lane. Upper-right equipment gallery has a near stair and a far staff door. Warm work lamps light hands; full-moon light edges blue timber, indigo rails, brass and red-and-gold parade decoration. The guest-facing viewing rope stays separate from the working rail. A clear near-side outer route lets crew move between costume bench and work stand without cutting across the loop or the guest line.
[MBeat] Location CH03 L02 Clown costume bench
[MMeta] Pale bench outside the rehearsal rail, picture and small paper pattern on a rear stand, portable lamp on a stable rear corner after CH03 01B A. Clown stands on the open opposite side; children use clear near and end edges. Red-and-blue costume trolley parks level alongside, brake applied. Fixed upper rack carries secured cushions above a low hinged costume box. Its lid can open without moving the cushions. Pattern labels describe garment lengths, not the clown’s height. The box contains one spare comic outfit, spare hat and closed stage-paint tin; the discarded sleeve is added later.
[MBeat] Location CH03 L03 Elephant work rail and safe stand
[MMeta] Blue decorative elephant body on a wheeled base inside the physical-right work bay. Blocks and thick temporary support restrain it until the final strap is fitted and inspected. Low model stand and ribbon bench are on the safe side of a fixed work rail. Replacement wheels are supported in a rack on the working side; only helpers lift them. Ground stop is reachable by Tara from a clear fixed platform beside the near gallery stair. Costume trolley later parks on level paving beside the near work-bay edge; a short loading board slopes towards that bay but ends outside the blocked elephant wheels. Its brake and side handle remain reachable from level paving. The final strap follows its marked path; a length match alone does not establish structural safety.
[MBeat] Location CH03 L04 Gallery and simultaneous yard action
[MMeta] Upper-right gallery overlooks the same work bay. Near stair has a fixed handrail; the gallery’s far door opens to the rear walkway and has a bolt on its far side. Veer’s drive control is beside the gallery rail; ground stop is below. Keep upper and lower actions in separate views. The broad helper stays below; the parade helper remains at the stop during Tara’s first pursuit. One plain escort is sent towards the remote passage end; that instruction is not an already-completed seal of every route.
[MBeat] Location CH03 L05 Counting arch and rear staff walkway
[MMeta] Far arch leads from the protected lane into Moon Square. Front reader and one silver slotted strip share a casing fixed at Navya’s waist height on the ground-level arch pier. Its rear cover opens into a recess hidden from the yard, accessible around the pier from safe paving. Upper rear walkway connects the bolted gallery door to a separate ticket-operated staff door. Existing near staff stair links walkway and recess: Veer descends to steal the strip, then returns upstairs before revealing it. This stair and the other approach serve Tara’s second pursuit without crossing the guest lane. Keep the same casing, slot and rear cover in every view. Writing shelf and message perch remain at the arch base.
[MBeat] Location CH03 L06 Moon Square board and fixed paving
[MMeta] From the Parade Arch: Clock Tower and Report Court left, Games Lane right, Grand Pavilion ahead. Pale paving surrounds low stage and three seating areas. Curved blue screen shelters one waiting group. Board and its accessible stop stand beside fixed outer paving, with a fixed shelf for the register. A folding bench can obstruct an inner path but never the preserved outer exit lane. First forty and next twenty-two make sixty-two guests; board stays at 40 after theft. Thirty are already seated, twelve by board, twenty behind screen. Ira and Kabir belong to twelve, girl in green and friends to twenty. No new signs displaying these subgroup answers yet.
[MScene] Teaching insertion map
[MMeta] T3.1 — Introduction, bus puzzle, complete Section 3.1 and Practice 3A.
[MMeta] T3.2 — Complete Section 3.2, Knowledge Building Task, Examples 5–8, Let’s Bake and Practice 3B.
[MMeta] T3.3A — Direct proportion, Example 9 and its complete Your turn. Stop before Example 10.
[MMeta] T3.3B — Example 10 and both methods, its Your turn, all Practice 3C and the Golden Ratio performance task.
[MMeta] R03 — Key Ideas, all nine Revision questions and solutions, complete nineteen-point Self-Check and final reflection.

[MScene] Scene CH03 01B The clown needs a sleeve
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L02 Clown costume bench. Pale bench outside the rehearsal rail, picture and small paper pattern on a rear stand, portable lamp on a stable rear corner after CH03 01B A. Clown stands on the open opposite side; children use clear near and end edges. Red-and-blue costume trolley parks level alongside, brake applied. Fixed upper rack carries secured cushions above a low hinged costume box. Its lid can open without moving the cushions. Pattern labels describe garment lengths, not the clown’s height. The box contains one spare comic outfit, spare hat and closed stage-paint tin; the discarded sleeve is added later.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Ahana at the near bench corner; Navya beside the pattern stand; Vihaan one step back; Tez below the near edge. The clown stands on the opposite, open side of the bench. Tara and the parade helper remain at the elephant; the broad helper is collecting the forty.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. The costume trolley is parked level beside the bench, brake applied, box closed, cushions secured above. Pattern and picture are visible but figures become readable only in the later insert. No spare outfit or stage paint is revealed yet.
[MMeta] Planning. Show only the frozen moment below. Use the beat’s explicit changes to pose or object state while retaining all other scene conditions. Do not illustrate future answers, repairs or visitor movements early.

SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE005-PANEL02; assigned main mathematics only No character or scenic background is required behind the main mathematics. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Who fits on the bus A bus is licensed to carry 1 bus captain and 30 sitting adult passengers. For its passenger capacity, 3 school children count as 2 adults. How many buses are needed for 40 adults and 40 children? We have to follow that capacity rule. Counting 80 people and treating every passenger as an adult would miss the clue about children. Let’s work it through Could 2 buses be enough? Together they have space for 2 × 30 = 60 adults. The 40 adults use 40 of those spaces, leaving 60 − 40 = 20 adult spaces. Every 2 adult spaces can take 3 children. There are 20 ÷ 2 = 10 such groups of spaces, so they can take 10 × 3 = 30 children. We have 40 children. Ten are still waiting! So 2 buses cannot carry everyone. Here is one way to fit everyone into 3 buses: Bus Adult passengers School children 1 30 0 2 10 30 3 0 10 DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V01. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. One possible arrangement using 3 buses Production note V01 — Solution table: preserve the three allocations 30/0, 10/30, 0/10. Counts total 40 adults and 40 children. Each bus has its own captain, separate from passenger capacity. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. On Bus 2, the 30 children count as 20 adults, so its load is 10 + 20 = 30 adult spaces. Bus 3 is comfortably within its limit. Each bus also has its own captain. We need at least 3 buses, and this arrangement shows that 3 work. END PANEL CH03-PAGE005-PANEL02 |

| SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 — 3.1 Introduction to Ratio NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE006-PANEL01; assigned teaching fields Teaching headings, explanations, examples, questions, working, answers, tables, diagrams and caption sentences are reader-facing. SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE006-PANEL01; assigned main mathematics only No character or scenic background is required behind the main mathematics. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT 3.1 Introduction to Ratio Let’s make the comparison easier to read. Eddy takes 10 shots at goal and scores 3 goals. Put the quantity named first on the left: shots at goal : goals scored = 10 : 3 Read 10 : 3 as “ten to three”. The two dots, called a colon, separate the numbers we are comparing. Those 3 goals came from the 10 shots; they are not 3 extra shots. A newspaper has 4 pages of sports news and 9 pages of entertainment news. Sports comes first in the comparison, so: sports pages : entertainment pages = 4 : 9 This kind of comparison is called a ratio. A ratio can compare two or more quantities. If we call two quantities A and B, we write their ratio as A : B. The letters tell us which quantities we mean. END PANEL CH03-PAGE006-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Example 1 Ali has a mass of 52 kg. Ying has a mass of 47 kg. Find the ratio of Ali’s mass to Ying’s mass. Let’s work it through Say the names in order: Ali, then Ying. Now write their masses in that same order. Both masses are in kilograms, so we can compare the numbers directly. Ali : Ying = 52 kg : 47 kg = 52 : 47 Once the units match, we leave them out of the ratio. If we reverse the names, we must reverse the numbers too: Ying : Ali = 47 : 52. The numbers have not changed places for fun; the question has changed. END PANEL CH03-PAGE006-PANEL02 |

| SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 — Ratios Involving Fractions and Decimals NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE008-PANEL02; assigned teaching fields Teaching headings, explanations, examples, questions, working, answers, tables, diagrams and caption sentences are reader-facing. Supplied paragraphs styled Production Note remain non-printing. Technical V identifiers are separated from figure captions into metadata. Every question, required visual and answer remains in source order; question blanks stay blank until the solution. SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE008-PANEL02; assigned teaching pause The teacher addresses the reader directly. Lessons do not move characters, consume dangerous story time or repair equipment. SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE008-PANEL02; assigned main mathematics only No character or scenic background is required behind the main mathematics. Keep question figures and intentional blanks distinct from completed answers. This block does not perform the story calculation for the characters. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Ratios Involving Fractions and Decimals The numbers in a ratio are called its terms. In simplest form, the terms are whole numbers with no common factor greater than 1. A common factor is a whole number that divides both terms exactly. For example, 2 : 3 is in simplest form. We cannot divide both 2 and 3 by the same whole number greater than 1 and still get whole numbers. What about 2.5 : 3? Think of two lengths measured in the same unit. Count them in half-unit pieces: 2.5 has 5 halves and 3 has 6 halves. The comparison becomes 5 : 6. In numbers: 2.5 : 3 = (2.5 × 2) : (3 × 2) = 5 : 6 We multiplied both terms by 2. We changed the size of the pieces we counted, while keeping the comparison the same. We’ll explore that idea further in Section 3.2. END PANEL CH03-PAGE008-PANEL02 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Example 3 Write the ratio 0.7 : 0.8 in its simplest form. Let’s work it through Look at the strips. Every small piece is 0.1. The amount 0.7 has 7 of these pieces; 0.8 has 8. Counting the same-sized pieces gives 7 : 8.  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V03. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. Seven tenths compared with eight tenths Production note V03 — Solution model: two equal-length wholes, each split into ten equal cells. Shade seven cells in the upper strip and eight in the lower strip. Label the rows 0.7 and 0.8 and state each cell is 0.1. Keep cell widths equal across rows. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. 0.7 : 0.8 = (0.7 × 10) : (0.8 × 10) = 7 : 8 There is no common factor greater than 1, so 7 : 8 is the simplest form. Notice that both terms were multiplied by 10. The same comparison tells us that 0.7 is 7/8 of 0.8, and 0.8 is 8/7 of 0.7. A ratio answer keeps both terms: write 7 : 8. The fraction 7/8 tells us how large the first amount is compared with the second. END PANEL CH03-PAGE009-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Example 4 A carton contains 1/2 L of orange juice. Another contains 5/8 L of apple juice. Find the ratio of the volume of orange juice to the volume of apple juice. Let’s work it through Keep orange first. Both volumes are in litres, so the ratio is 1/2 : 5/8. Let’s count equal eighth-litre portions instead.  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V04. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. Use the same eighth-litre portion for both juices. Production note V04 — Solution model: two equal one-litre bars split into eight equal parts. Upper orange-juice bar has four orange cells and label 1/2 L = 4/8 L. Lower apple-juice bar has five teal cells and label 5/8 L. Label each cell 1/8 L. Unfilled cells remain white. Names and counts must work without colour. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. The orange juice fills 4 portions; the apple juice fills 5. Here is how multiplying both fractions by 8 counts those portions: 1/2 × 8 = (1 × 8)/2 = 8 ÷ 2 = 4 5/8 × 8 = (5 × 8)/8 = 40 ÷ 8 = 5 orange juice : apple juice = 4 : 5 The drawing also shows that the orange volume is 4/5 of the apple volume. The apple volume is 5/4 of the orange volume. Keep track of which juice you are comparing with which. END PANEL CH03-PAGE010-PANEL01 |

Those two small checks prevent quite a few large muddles. Concept Building Questions Practice 3A Question 1 Sam has saved $53 and Mali has saved $65. Find the ratio of Sam’s savings to Mali’s savings. Check your method Sam : Mali = $53 : $65 = 53 : 65 Both savings use dollars. The ratio is already in simplest form. Check your method Haris : Han = 161 cm : 158 cm = 161 : 158 END PANEL CH03-PAGE012-PANEL01 |

| SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 — Practice 3A Question 3 NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE012-PANEL02; assigned teaching fields Teaching headings, explanations, examples, questions, working, answers, tables, diagrams and caption sentences are reader-facing. SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE012-PANEL02; assigned main mathematics only No character or scenic background is required behind the main mathematics. Keep question figures and intentional blanks distinct from completed answers. This block does not perform the story calculation for the characters. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Practice 3A Question 3 Write each ratio in its simplest form. (a) 0.85 m : 73 cm     (b) 1/9 : 7/9 (c) 1/4 h : 17 min     (d) 7.83 : 82.1 Check your method (a) The units differ. Since 1 m = 100 cm, change 0.85 m to centimetres first: 0.85 × 100 = 85 cm. 0.85 m : 73 cm = 85 cm : 73 cm = 85 : 73 We changed only the metre measurement because we were converting its unit. Once units match, simplifying a ratio means changing both terms together. (b) Both fractions count ninths. Multiply each by 9: (1/9 × 9) : (7/9 × 9) = (9 ÷ 9) : (63 ÷ 9) = 1 : 7 (c) One hour has 60 minutes. A quarter of an hour is 60 ÷ 4 = 15 minutes. 1/4 h : 17 min = 15 min : 17 min = 15 : 17 (d) There are two decimal places in 7.83. Multiply both terms by 100; writing 82.1 as 82.10 helps us see both changes. 7.83 : 82.10 = (7.83 × 100) : (82.10 × 100) = 783 : 8210 The whole numbers 783 and 8210 have no common factor greater than 1, so we stop there. END PANEL CH03-PAGE012-PANEL02 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Practice 3A Question 5 Find the ratio of the travelling times of (a) the flight from Singapore to Manila to the flight from Singapore to Penang and (b) the flight from Singapore to Manila to the flight from Singapore to Bangkok. From Singapore to Travelling time Manila 3 h 45 min Penang 1 h 15 min Bangkok 2 h 5 min Production note V06 — Question data: preserve the three destinations and exact mixed-unit times. Do not print converted minutes or answers. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. Check your method An hour has 60 minutes, so 3 h 45 min is not 3.45 hours. Change the hours to minutes, then add the minutes already given: Manila: 3 × 60 + 45 = 180 + 45 = 225 min Penang: 1 × 60 + 15 = 75 min Bangkok: 2 × 60 + 5 = 125 min (a) Divide both terms by 75: 225 : 75 = (225 ÷ 75) : (75 ÷ 75) = 3 : 1 (b) Divide both terms by 25: 225 : 125 = (225 ÷ 25) : (125 ÷ 25) = 9 : 5 END PANEL CH03-PAGE013-PANEL02 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Practice 3A Question 6 Simon’s height is 1.67 m and his weight is 43 kg. He claims that the ratio of his height to his weight is 1.67 m : 43 kg. Explain whether he is correct. Check your method Simon is not making a same-kind comparison: 1.67 m measures length, while 43 kg measures mass. Changing metres to centimetres would still leave length compared with mass. We cannot turn both into one matching unit. For the ratios we are simplifying here, compare lengths with lengths or masses with masses. Comparisons between different kinds, such as kilometres per litre, keep their units and need a different interpretation. END PANEL CH03-PAGE014-PANEL01 |
| PANEL CH03-PAGE014-PANEL02 |
| SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 — Practice 3A Question 7 NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE014-PANEL02; assigned teaching fields Teaching headings, explanations, examples, questions, working, answers, tables, diagrams and caption sentences are reader-facing. SOURCE: Teaching block T3.1 APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE014-PANEL02; assigned main mathematics only No character or scenic background is required behind the main mathematics. Keep question figures and intentional blanks distinct from completed answers. This block does not perform the story calculation for the characters. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Practice 3A Question 7 Joseph and Nurul have numbers of balls in the ratio 2 : 3. They are each given an equal number of extra balls. They claim that their ratio is still 2 : 3. (a) Is this claim possible? Use specific numbers to support your answer. (b) What do you learn from this? Check your method (a) Suppose Joseph starts with 2 balls and Nurul with 3. Give each 1 more ball. Joseph now has 2 + 1 = 3; Nurul has 3 + 1 = 4. The new ratio is 3 : 4, not 2 : 3. Their difference stays 1 ball. To have the ratio 2 : 3 with more balls, both amounts would have to grow by the same factor: for example, 4 : 6 has a difference of 2 balls. Equal additions cannot do that. If both receive some extra balls, their 2 : 3 comparison changes. (b) Adding the same number to both terms does not generally preserve a ratio. Multiplying or dividing both terms by the same positive number does. Let’s now see why that works. END PANEL CH03-PAGE014-PANEL02 |

TEACHING COPY BEGINS — Production notes remain non-printing 3.2 Equivalent Ratios Look at the wheel groups. Each motorcycle has 2 wheels and each car has 4. We are counting all the wheels, including car wheels that a side-on picture might hide.  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V07. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. Count wheels, rather than vehicles. Production note V07 — Teaching figure: three outlined motorcycle groups with exactly two wheel symbols per group and three car groups with exactly four wheel symbols per group. Label each group Motorcycle or Car; all wheels must be individually visible. The totals are six and twelve, but vehicle counts are three and three. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. motorcycle wheels : car wheels = (3 × 2) : (3 × 4) = 6 : 12 For every 6 motorcycle wheels there are 12 car wheels. We can describe the same comparison with smaller numbers. The next activity will show what stays the same. END PANEL CH03-PAGE016-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Check your method  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V09. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. More divisions, but the same white-and-red comparison Production note V09 — Completed answer to activity 3: three equal-total-length bars partitioned into 9, 12 and 18 equal cells. White/red counts exactly 3/6, 4/8 and 6/12; ratio labels shown at the right. Colour boundary remains at one-third, aligned vertically across bars. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. The white part fills the same share of every strip, and the red part fills the same share too. In each strip, the red length is twice the white length. More dividing lines have not changed that comparison. Write three other ratios equivalent to 1 : 2. What do you observe about their numbers? One possible answer 1 : 2 = (1 × 5) : (2 × 5) = 5 : 10 1 : 2 = (1 × 7) : (2 × 7) = 7 : 14 1 : 2 = (1 × 10) : (2 × 10) = 10 : 20 The second term is twice the first in every pair. Other answers are possible: use the same positive multiplier on both terms. Ratios that describe the same comparison are called equivalent ratios. The bars showed why 1 : 2, 2 : 4, 3 : 6, 4 : 8 and 6 : 12 are equivalent. To simplify, divide both terms by a common factor. You can do this in stages: 4 : 8 = (4 ÷ 2) : (8 ÷ 2) = 2 : 4 2 : 4 = (2 ÷ 2) : (4 ÷ 2) = 1 : 2 6 : 12 = (6 ÷ 3) : (12 ÷ 3) = 2 : 4 = 1 : 2 Stop when the terms are whole numbers with no common factor greater than 1. Here that gives 1 : 2. Dividing only one term would change the comparison, rather like shrinking only one shoe in a pair. END PANEL CH03-PAGE018-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Example 5 Write each ratio in its simplest form: (a) 8 : 14 and (b) 14 : 4. Let’s work it through (a) Both 8 and 14 divide exactly by 2: 8 : 14 = (8 ÷ 2) : (14 ÷ 2) = 4 : 7 The terms 4 and 7 have no common factor greater than 1. That is our simplest ratio. (b) Both terms divide by 2 again: 14 : 4 = (14 ÷ 2) : (4 ÷ 2) = 7 : 2 The first term can be larger than the second. Keep their order. A calculator with a fraction feature can check this. Enter 8 as the top number and 14 as the bottom number. If it displays the simplified fraction 4/7, write the ratio as 4 : 7. For 14/4, the simplified fraction is 7/2, giving 7 : 2.  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V10. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. A fraction display can help you check a ratio. Production note V10 — Illustrative generic calculator with recognisable body, display and number keys. Display input 8/14 and simplified result 4/7 in stacked fraction notation; label fraction function. Add text 8:14 becomes 4:7 beside it. Do not imply a universal key sequence; exact key positions vary. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. Is 8 : 14 equivalent to 14 : 8? Check your thinking 8 : 14 = 4 : 7;     14 : 8 = (14 ÷ 2) : (8 ÷ 2) = 7 : 4 No. In 4 : 7 the first amount is smaller; in 7 : 4 it is larger. Reversing the order changes the comparison. END PANEL CH03-PAGE019-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Example 7 Thiru and John worked together on a project. Thiru worked for 15 hours and John for 9 hours. They shared their payment in the ratio 8 : 6. Was the payment fair? Let’s work it through Here we’ll treat fair payment as the same pay for each hour of the same kind of work. Then the payment should be shared in the same ratio as the hours. hours, Thiru : John = 15 : 9 = (15 ÷ 3) : (9 ÷ 3) = 5 : 3 payment, Thiru : John = 8 : 6 = (8 ÷ 2) : (6 ÷ 2) = 4 : 3 The simplest ratios are 5 : 3 and 4 : 3. They do not match, so the payment was not fair on an equal-pay-per-hour basis. A share of 5 : 3 would match their working time. END PANEL CH03-PAGE021-PANEL01 |

Let’s work it through The ratio 6 : 4 tells us how to share the sweets. It does not say Tony gets only 6 sweets and Rudy only 4. Think of 6 equal parts for Tony and 4 equal parts for Rudy.  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V11. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. The 20 sweets fill 10 equal parts. Production note V11 — Solution bar model: exactly ten equal cells; first six teal labelled Tony, last four gold labelled Rudy. Brace the complete bar as 20 sweets. Each cell contains two sweet symbols; total count exactly 20. Group labels six parts and four parts distinguish ratio terms from actual sweet counts. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. Total parts = 6 + 4 = 10 Sweets in 1 part = 20 ÷ 10 = 2 Now multiply each number of parts by 2: Tony: 6 × 2 = 12 sweets;     Rudy: 4 × 2 = 8 sweets Check both conditions: 12 + 8 = 20 sweets, and 12 : 8 = (12 ÷ 2) : (8 ÷ 2) = 6 : 4. The total and the sharing rule both fit. END PANEL CH03-PAGE022-PANEL01 |

Find Eva’s score for Paper 2. Student Paper 1 Paper 2 James 45 60 Eva 36 ? Production note V14 — Question data: James 45 and 60, Eva 36 and unknown. Preserve separate paper columns and the unknown; do not insert 48 here. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. Check your method Here 60 is James’ score, not their combined score. It matches James’ 5 parts. So divide by 5, not by 5 + 4: 5 parts = 60 marks;     1 part = 60 ÷ 5 = 12 marks Eva’s 4 parts = 4 × 12 = 48 marks Paper 1 follows the same ratio: 45 : 36 = (45 ÷ 9) : (36 ÷ 9) = 5 : 4. For Paper 2, 60 : 48 = 5 : 4 too. END PANEL CH03-PAGE027-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Practice 3B Question 4 Tim worked for 20 hours and was paid $100. Peter worked for 12 hours and was paid $60. (a) Find the ratio of Tim’s working hours to Peter’s. (b) Find the ratio of Tim’s salary to Peter’s. (c) Explain why the salaries are fair. Check your method (a) 20 : 12 = (20 ÷ 4) : (12 ÷ 4) = 5 : 3 (b) 100 : 60 = (100 ÷ 20) : (60 ÷ 20) = 5 : 3 (c) The ratios match. Tim receives $100 ÷ 20 = $5 per hour, and Peter receives $60 ÷ 12 = $5 per hour. They receive equal pay per hour for the same kind of work. END PANEL CH03-PAGE027-PANEL02 |

Check your method (a) School A: 800 : 40 = (800 ÷ 40) : (40 ÷ 40) = 20 : 1 (b) School B: 1600 : 90 = (1600 ÷ 10) : (90 ÷ 10) = 160 : 9 (c) I would choose School B for its lower number of students per teacher. Compare both schools using 9 teachers: School A: 20 : 1 = (20 × 9) : (1 × 9) = 180 : 9 School B: 160 : 9 For 9 teachers, School B has 160 students compared with School A’s 180. That may allow more attention per student. These are school-wide averages, not actual class sizes. END PANEL CH03-PAGE028-PANEL02 |

The box contains one spare comic outfit, spare hat and closed stage-paint tin; the discarded sleeve is added later.

[MScene] Scene CH03 03A The first forty pass the arch
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L01 Parade Ground master. View from Food Street towards the counting arch: protected pedestrian lane follows the physical-left edge; central rehearsal loop is empty; elephant work bay is on the physical right. Costume bench is outside the loop beside the near part of the lane. Upper-right equipment gallery has a near stair and a far staff door. Warm work lamps light hands; full-moon light edges blue timber, indigo rails, brass and red-and-gold parade decoration. The guest-facing viewing rope stays separate from the working rail. A clear near-side outer route lets crew move between costume bench and work stand without cutting across the loop or the guest line.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Broad helper leads the first group along the left pedestrian lane. Seating helpers wait beyond the far arch. The trio, Tara and clown remain at the work areas, off-frame in the arch insert.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. First forty only. Their invitations activate the still-installed counting strip. The second twenty-two have not crossed the arch. Do not draw an exact forty-person crowd in a small image; the narration supplies the total.
[MMeta] Planning. Show only the frozen moment below. Use the beat’s explicit changes to pose or object state while retaining all other scene conditions. Do not illustrate future answers, repairs or visitor movements early.

Clown stands on the open opposite side; children use clear near and end edges. Red-and-blue costume trolley parks level alongside, brake applied. Fixed upper rack carries secured cushions above a low hinged costume box. Its lid can open without moving the cushions. Pattern labels describe garment lengths, not the clown’s height. The box contains one spare comic outfit, spare hat and closed stage-paint tin; the discarded sleeve is added later.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Clown crouches at his trolley’s low box; trio watch from the bench side. Ira and Kabir stay behind the viewing rope. Tara returns towards the work rail.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. The box is beneath a fixed upper cushion rack. Its low hinged lid can open without lifting the secured cushions. It has a simple catch, not a lock; the later jolt can release an incompletely caught lid. Trolley brake is applied on level paving.

[MScene] Scene CH03 04A A model to measure by
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L03 Elephant work rail and safe stand. Blue decorative elephant body on a wheeled base inside the physical-right work bay. Blocks and thick temporary support restrain it until the final strap is fitted and inspected. Low model stand and ribbon bench are on the safe side of a fixed work rail. Replacement wheels are supported in a rack on the working side; only helpers lift them. Ground stop is reachable by Tara from a clear fixed platform beside the near gallery stair. Costume trolley later parks on level paving beside the near work-bay edge; a short loading board slopes towards that bay but ends outside the blocked elephant wheels. Its brake and side handle remain reachable from level paving. The final strap follows its marked path; a length match alone does not establish structural safety.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Tara and the broad and parade helpers on the working side. Trio and Tez on the safe side of the low work stand. Ira and Kabir behind the adjacent viewing rope.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. The float is still blocked and temporarily strapped. Candidate wheels are held in a rack behind the rail; children cannot lift them. Model body 60 cm, actual body 300 cm, model wheel diameter 8 cm and model strap path 30 cm. No matching-part answers yet. Before arriving here, Ahana carries the rolled measuring tape from the costume bench and places it on this safe work stand. It remains there until CH03 06A D; her hands are free for the intervening work.
[MMeta] Planning. Show only the frozen moment below. Use the beat’s explicit changes to pose or object state while retaining all other scene conditions. Do not illustrate future answers, repairs or visitor movements early.

Replacement wheels are supported in a rack on the working side; only helpers lift them. Ground stop is reachable by Tara from a clear fixed platform beside the near gallery stair. Costume trolley later parks on level paving beside the near work-bay edge; a short loading board slopes towards that bay but ends outside the blocked elephant wheels. Its brake and side handle remain reachable from level paving. The final strap follows its marked path; a length match alone does not establish structural safety.

[PGContext] Each visual beat specifies one frozen image. Earlier or later actions explicitly placed between images are continuity context, not a request for multiple bodies or poses in one picture. A source caption may describe several actions across the illustrated sequence. Silent beats show necessary handovers, setup or intermediate gestures without invented dialogue.

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT PRINT FRIENDLY TEACHER BRIDGE The elephant needs matching parts, not a bigger guess. Let’s see how quantities grow together. TEACHING COPY BEGINS — Production notes remain non-printing 3.3 Direct Proportion When Brenda made enough cake for three times as many people, she used three times as much of every ingredient. Keeping the same recipe kept the proportions the same. Both amounts changed by the same factor: 4 × 3 = 12 people, and 3 × 3 = 9 cups of flour. This is direct proportion. The amount for one person stays the same. Let’s use that “one first” idea with cookies. END PANEL CH03-PAGE038-PANEL01 |
| PANEL CH03-PAGE038-PANEL02 |
| SOURCE: Teaching block T3.3A — Example 9 NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE038-PANEL02; assigned teaching fields Teaching headings, explanations, examples, questions, working, answers, tables, diagrams and caption sentences are reader-facing. Supplied paragraphs styled Production Note remain non-printing. Technical V identifiers are separated from figure captions into metadata. SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE038-PANEL02; assigned teaching pause The teacher addresses the reader directly. Lessons do not move characters, consume dangerous story time or repair equipment. SOURCE: Teaching block T3.3A APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE038-PANEL02; assigned main mathematics only No character or scenic background is required behind the main mathematics. Keep question figures and intentional blanks distinct from completed answers. This block does not perform the story calculation for the characters. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Example 9 Ahmad uses 128 g of oatmeal to make 16 oatmeal cookies. How much oatmeal does he need to make 24 oatmeal cookies? Let’s work it through We are making cookies of the same size using the same recipe. So each cookie needs the same amount of oatmeal. First find that amount for one cookie, then make 24 of those equal amounts.  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V15. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. Divide both quantities by 16, then multiply both by 24. Production note V15 — Solution diagram: two aligned columns headed Cookies and Oatmeal. Rows 16/128 g, 1/8 g and 24/192 g. Between first and second rows place ÷16 arrows on both columns; between second and third place ×24 arrows on both. Include one recognisable cookie beside the middle row, not an uncounted decorative group. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. Oatmeal for 1 cookie = 128 ÷ 16 = 8 g Oatmeal for 24 cookies = 8 × 24 = 192 g Ahmad needs 192 g of oatmeal. Check: 192 ÷ 24 = 8 g per cookie, the same as 128 ÷ 16. More cookies need more oatmeal, but each cookie’s share stays 8 g. Finding one item first is often called the unitary method. “One first” is the idea to remember. Direct proportion needs that unchanged amount per item; growing older, for instance, does not make your height double whenever your age doubles. END PANEL CH03-PAGE038-PANEL02 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Your turn after Example 9 David exchanged 300 Euros and received 420 SGD. If he exchanged 6000 Euros, how much SGD would he receive? Check your method Use the same exchange rate, with no extra fees. SGD means Singapore dollars. Find the amount for 1 Euro first: 1 Euro gives 420 ÷ 300 = 1.4 SGD. 6000 Euros give 6000 × 1.4 = 8400 SGD. Check the scale: 6000 ÷ 300 = 20, and 420 × 20 = 8400. Both currency amounts have been multiplied by 20. END PANEL CH03-PAGE039-PANEL01 |

Costume trolley later parks on level paving beside the near work-bay edge; a short loading board slopes towards that bay but ends outside the blocked elephant wheels. Its brake and side handle remain reachable from level paving. The final strap follows its marked path; a length match alone does not establish structural safety.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Trio at a safe ribbon bench beside the model stand. Clown brings the ribbon box on the pedestrian side. Helpers change wheels behind the rail; Tara supervises there. Ira and Kabir stay at the viewing rope.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. Use an empty-drive, blocked float during wheel work. The thirty ribbons are initially plain; blue and gold are their intended colours. The ribbon box is distinct from the costume box beneath the trolley. Before this scene, the clown brings his light costume trolley along the clear near-side outer route to the ribbon stand, parks it level beside the short loading board and applies its brake. Its box stays closed and cushions secured. The near-side route remains clear for the children.

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT PRINT FRIENDLY TEACHER BRIDGE Thirty ribbons, two colours, one sharing rule. Let’s work out what each part is worth. TEACHING COPY BEGINS — Production notes remain non-printing Example 10 Sara and Nora used their savings to buy a storybook. They shared the cost in the ratio 4 : 5. The book cost $18. How much did each pay? Let’s work it through The $18 is the whole cost. Sara has 4 parts and Nora has 5, so there are 4 + 5 = 9 equal parts altogether. We’ll show two ways to organise the same sharing calculation. Method 1 Scale the ratio The total changes from 9 parts to $18, so the multiplier is 18 ÷ 9 = 2. Use that multiplier in every row: Who Parts Multiply by 2 Amount Sara 4 4 × $2 $8 Nora 5 5 × $2 $10 Total 9 9 × $2 $18 Production note V16 — Source method 1: aligned rows Sara 4→$8, Nora 5→$10, total 9→$18; multiplier two on each row. Total 9 derives from 4+5. This is a solved teaching table. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. Method 2 Find the value of one part Look at the bar. The whole $18 fills 9 equal boxes. Four boxes belong to Sara and five to Nora.  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V17. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. The whole bar is $18, not either person’s share. Production note V17 — Source method 2: exactly nine equal cells, four white for Sara and five yellow for Nora; labelled braces mark each group and one brace marks the full $18. Do not label the four-cell group as the whole. Keep labels outside the cells. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. 1 part = $18 ÷ 9 = $2 Sara: 4 × $2 = $8;     Nora: 5 × $2 = $10 Sara pays $8 and Nora pays $10. Both methods find the same $2 per part. Check: $8 + $10 = $18, and 8 : 10 = (8 ÷ 2) : (10 ÷ 2) = 4 : 5. END PANEL CH03-PAGE042-PANEL01 |

Practice 3C Question 1 A fruit punch is made by mixing orange juice and apple juice in the ratio 2 : 3. Darla needs 20 L of fruit punch for a party. How much of each juice must she use? Check your method Total parts = 2 + 3 = 5;     1 part = 20 ÷ 5 = 4 L Orange juice: 2 × 4 = 8 L;     apple juice: 3 × 4 = 12 L The volumes add to 8 + 12 = 20 L. The 2 : 3 compares the two juices; orange juice is 2 of the 5 total parts, not 2 of 3 total parts. Practice 3C Question 2 Siti and Nora shared $100 in the ratio 2 : 3. How much did each girl receive? Check your method Total parts = 2 + 3 = 5;     1 part = $100 ÷ 5 = $20 Siti: 2 × $20 = $40;     Nora: 3 × $20 = $60 END PANEL CH03-PAGE043-PANEL02 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Practice 3C Question 3 Ameera exchanged 750 UAE Dirham and received 200 USD. How much is 1 UAE Dirham in USD? Give your answer to the nearest cent. Check your method USD means US dollars. Divide the 200 dollars by 750 to find the value of 1 dirham at this exchange rate: 200 ÷ 750 = 200/750 = 4/15 = 0.2666… USD One cent is $0.01, so we need two decimal places. In 0.2666…, the third decimal digit is 6. Round 0.26 up to 0.27. 1 UAE Dirham ≈ 0.27 USD, or 27 US cents. The ≈ sign means “approximately equal to”. Keep the unrounded value in any further calculation; 0.27 is the rounded answer requested here. END PANEL CH03-PAGE044-PANEL01 |

SOURCE: Teaching block T3.3B APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE045-PANEL01; assigned main mathematics only No character or scenic background is required behind the main mathematics. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Practice 3C Question 5 Car A is advertised as using 3 litres of petrol for 38 km. Car B is advertised as using 2 litres for 25 km. Which car would you choose? Explain your answer. Check your method For fuel efficiency, compare the distances travelled on the same amount of petrol. Finding the distance for 1 litre gives: Car A: 38 ÷ 3 = 12 2/3 km per litre Car B: 25 ÷ 2 = 12 1/2 km per litre For Car A, 3 × 12 = 36, leaving 2 km to share among 3 litres. That gives another 2/3 km per litre. For Car B, 2 × 12 = 24, leaving 1/2 km per litre. The whole parts are both 12. Compare the extra fractions using sixths: 2/3 = (2 × 2)/(3 × 2) = 4/6;     1/2 = (1 × 3)/(2 × 3) = 3/6 Since 4/6 is greater than 3/6, Car A travels farther per litre. I would choose Car A for its better advertised fuel efficiency. We are choosing on the given fuel figures; the question gives no other features to compare. END PANEL CH03-PAGE045-PANEL01 |

| SOURCE: Teaching block T3.3B — Performance Task Golden Ratio NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE046-PANEL01; assigned teaching fields Teaching headings, explanations, examples, questions, working, answers, tables, diagrams and caption sentences are reader-facing. SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE046-PANEL01; assigned teaching pause The teacher addresses the reader directly. Lessons do not move characters, consume dangerous story time or repair equipment. SOURCE: Teaching block T3.3B APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE046-PANEL01; assigned main mathematics only No character or scenic background is required behind the main mathematics. Keep question figures and intentional blanks distinct from completed answers. This block does not perform the story calculation for the characters. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Performance Task Golden Ratio Some comparisons have special names. Two amounts are in the golden ratio when the larger divided by the smaller is approximately 1.618. We write larger : smaller ≈ 1.618 : 1. You may see claims that buildings at the Acropolis in Athens were designed using this ratio. Let’s check the evidence before treating that as a fact. Drawing a rectangle over a photograph does not prove that the builder planned to use this ratio. 1. Draw a rectangle whose longer side and shorter side follow the golden ratio. Choose a sensible shorter side, then work out a longer side about 1.618 times as long. Try your drawing before looking at mine. One possible drawing Choose a shorter side of 5 cm. The longer side should be about 5 × 1.618 = 8.09 cm. An ordinary millimetre ruler lets us mark about 8.1 cm, so our hand-drawn rectangle will be an approximation. Use 1 mm grid paper. Draw a horizontal line 8.1 cm long, starting your ruler at its 0 mark. From each end, follow the paper’s vertical grid lines up 5 cm. Join the two top ends. The grid helps keep all four corners square.  DO NOT PRINT — Figure reference V18. The following caption sentence is reader-facing. One approximate golden rectangle with sides 8.1 cm and 5 cm Production note V18 — Completed drawing answer: rectangle drawn at aspect ratio 8.1:5, width 8.1 cm and height 5 cm labelled, four right-angle marks, faint square grid. Label diagram not actual size and hand drawing approximate. The intended exact decimal calculation 8.09 cm is rounded to 8.1 cm for a millimetre ruler. Do not imply the printed figure can be measured at the labelled dimensions. Use the current ChandRush colour and design style wherever it supports understanding. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. Longer side ÷ shorter side = 8.1 ÷ 5 = 1.62 The value 1.62 is close to 1.618, so this is a suitable approximate drawing. Your rectangle can be a different size and still have the same proportions. END PANEL CH03-PAGE046-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT 2. Search the Internet to find out how the golden ratio is used in everyday life. Try the search “golden ratio design documented example”. Choose an example, name where you found it, and explain what the designer used the ratio for. Look for the designer’s own organisation, a museum or a university, rather than copying an attractive diagram without checking it. One researched example Architect Le Corbusier used the golden ratio in a system of measurements called the Modulor. It helped him choose proportions for buildings. His foundation describes this use in its “Corbusean vocabulary” page. This is a documented use in design, rather than a guess based only on how a building looks. You can investigate another example. Record its name, the website and the evidence of deliberate use. Not every rectangle or spiral follows the golden ratio; measuring and checking are part of the investigation. Research source for the sample: Fondation Le Corbusier, “Corbusean vocabulary”, section “Modulor”, accessed 19 October 2026: https://www.fondationlecorbusier.fr/en/thematic-folder/corbusean-vocabulary/. The sample is supplied as one possible research result; no student search or observation is claimed. END PANEL CH03-PAGE047-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT PRINT FRIENDLY TEACHER BRIDGE The parts are ready for their last check. Let’s check our ratio skills too. TEACHING COPY BEGINS — Production notes remain non-printing Chapter 3 Key Ideas Before the revision, let’s collect the methods with numbers beside them. Name the quantities in order Apples : oranges = 2 : 3 means 2 apple parts for every 3 orange parts. It does not tell us the total number of fruits. Reversing the order gives oranges : apples = 3 : 2. Simplify both terms together 6 : 3 = (6 ÷ 3) : (3 ÷ 3) = 2 : 1 Use whole-number terms with no common factor greater than 1. Multiplying or dividing both terms by the same positive number gives an equivalent ratio: 2 : 3 = (2 × 2) : (3 × 2) = 4 : 6 4 : 6 = (4 × 2) : (6 × 2) = 8 : 12 100 : 150 = (100 ÷ 10) : (150 ÷ 10) = 10 : 15 10 : 15 = (10 ÷ 5) : (15 ÷ 5) = 2 : 3 END PANEL CH03-PAGE049-PANEL01 |
| PANEL CH03-PAGE049-PANEL02 |
| SOURCE: Teaching block R03 — Match units before comparing NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT SOURCE: How to use this manuscript APPLIES TO: CH03-PAGE049-PANEL02; assigned teaching fields Teaching headings, explanations, examples, questions, working, answers, tables, diagrams and caption sentences are reader-facing. END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Match units before comparing Since 1 m = 100 cm: 6 cm : 1 m = 6 cm : 100 cm = 6 : 100 6 : 100 = (6 ÷ 2) : (100 ÷ 2) = 3 : 50 Know which amount the given number represents In a 2 : 3 comparison, suppose the 2 parts represent $30. Then 1 part is $30 ÷ 2 = $15, and 3 parts are 3 × $15 = $45. The $30 belongs to the first share; it is not the total. If instead a total is to be shared in the ratio 2 : 3, divide that total into 2 + 3 = 5 equal parts. Multiply the value of one part by 2 and by 3 to find the shares. For direct proportion, the amount per item stays the same. Find one item’s amount, then multiply by the required number of items. END PANEL CH03-PAGE049-PANEL02 |

(a) 25 : 35     (b) 8 : 120     (c) 9 m : 30 m     (d) 60 L : 85 L (e) 200 cm : 30 cm     (f) 5 kg : 1000 kg     (g) 7.8 : 4.8 Check your method (a) 25 : 35 = (25 ÷ 5) : (35 ÷ 5) = 5 : 7 (b) 8 : 120 = (8 ÷ 8) : (120 ÷ 8) = 1 : 15 (c) 9 m : 30 m = 9 : 30 = (9 ÷ 3) : (30 ÷ 3) = 3 : 10 (d) 60 L : 85 L = 60 : 85 = (60 ÷ 5) : (85 ÷ 5) = 12 : 17 (e) 200 cm : 30 cm = 200 : 30 = (200 ÷ 10) : (30 ÷ 10) = 20 : 3 (f) 5 kg : 1000 kg = 5 : 1000 = (5 ÷ 5) : (1000 ÷ 5) = 1 : 200 (g) First make both terms whole numbers, then simplify: 7.8 : 4.8 = (7.8 × 10) : (4.8 × 10) = 78 : 48 78 : 48 = (78 ÷ 6) : (48 ÷ 6) = 13 : 8 END PANEL CH03-PAGE050-PANEL01 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Revision Question 7 Jane worked for 2 full days and received $70. Alice worked for 3 full days and received $100. Were their salaries fair? Explain your answer. Check your method Assume their full working days were equally long and they did the same kind of work. Fair pay on that basis would mean the same amount for each day. working days, Jane : Alice = 2 : 3 pay, Jane : Alice = 70 : 100 = (70 ÷ 10) : (100 ÷ 10) = 7 : 10 The ratios 2 : 3 and 7 : 10 are not equivalent. Jane’s pay is $70 ÷ 2 = $35 per day. At that rate, Alice should receive 3 × $35 = $105, not $100. So their salaries were not fair on an equal-pay-per-day basis. END PANEL CH03-PAGE053-PANEL01 |

Lucy concludes that there are 4 apples and 5 oranges in the bag. Explain whether she is correct. If she is incorrect, what should her conclusion be? Check your method Lucy’s numbers are possible, but they are not the only possibility. A bag with 8 apples and 10 oranges also fits: 8 : 10 = (8 ÷ 2) : (10 ÷ 2) = 4 : 5 The ratio tells us “4 apples for every 5 oranges”. It does not tell us how many groups of 4 and 5 are in the bag. We need more information, such as the total number of fruits, to find the actual counts. END PANEL CH03-PAGE053-PANEL02 |

END NON-PRINTING CARRIED PRODUCTION CONTEXT ORIGINAL PANEL CONTENT Revision Question 9 Someone claims that 1/2 : 1/3 is equivalent to 2 : 3. Do you agree? If you disagree, how would you change the statement? Explain your answer. Check your method No. The 2 and 3 are the denominators, not the amounts being compared. Multiply both fractions by 6 to get whole-number terms: 1/2 × 6 = 6/2 = 3;     1/3 × 6 = 6/3 = 2 So 1/2 : 1/3 = 3 : 2, not 2 : 3. A quick size check helps: 1/2 is larger than 1/3, so the first term in our simplified ratio should be larger too. END PANEL CH03-PAGE054-PANEL01 |

Skill Revision questions My score Compare two quantities using a ratio and reduce a ratio to its simplest form 1(a–g), 3 ___ / 8 Find equivalent ratios 2(a–d) ___ / 4 Divide a quantity in a given ratio 4, 5, 6 ___ / 3 Solve word problems involving direct proportion 4, 5, 6, 7 ___ / 4 Total  ___ / 19 Production note V20 — Preserve source self-check mapping and denominators 8,4,3,4 with total 19. If that style would reduce clarity, retain the teaching colours, contrast and mathematical design required here. Mathematical accuracy and comprehension take priority. Questions 8 and 9 are extra reasoning checks. Can you explain why 4 : 5 does not give exact fruit counts, and why 1/2 : 1/3 becomes 3 : 2? Use the numbers in your explanation. If one skill needs another look, return to the matching worked example, cover its solution and try again. Being able to explain your next step is a better sign of understanding than remembering the final number. END PANEL CH03-PAGE055-PANEL01 |

[MScene] Scene CH03 06B Stop the unfinished float
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L04 Gallery and simultaneous yard action. Upper-right gallery overlooks the same work bay. Near stair has a fixed handrail; the gallery’s far door opens to the rear walkway and has a bolt on its far side. Veer’s drive control is beside the gallery rail; ground stop is below. Keep upper and lower actions in separate views. The broad helper stays below; the parade helper remains at the stop during Tara’s first pursuit. One plain escort is sent towards the remote passage end; that instruction is not an already-completed seal of every route.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Veer at the gallery control; Tara at the ground stop lever. Parade helper beside Tara. Broad helper beside the blocked float. Trio stay on safe stand side; clown beside costume trolley; guests behind rope.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. No one rides the float. Final strap is unfitted. The control starts only the drive and opens the rehearsal gate; it does not release blocks or temporary support. Ground stop cuts drive and is held down until the safe test.

[MScene] Scene CH03 06D The bolted door above
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L04 Gallery and simultaneous yard action. Upper-right gallery overlooks the same work bay. Near stair has a fixed handrail; the gallery’s far door opens to the rear walkway and has a bolt on its far side. Veer’s drive control is beside the gallery rail; ground stop is below. Keep upper and lower actions in separate views. The broad helper stays below; the parade helper remains at the stop during Tara’s first pursuit. One plain escort is sent towards the remote passage end; that instruction is not an already-completed seal of every route.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Tara alone at the gallery’s far door on the yard side. Veer just inside the rear walkway. Parade helper remains below at stop; broad helper at float. One separate plain escort is available to watch the remote passage end.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. Alternate above with the still-unresolved trolley below. Veer’s service ticket is pocketed, walking stick right. The first door’s bolt is physical and operated from its far side; a ticket does not erase that bolt.
[MMeta] Planning. Show only the frozen moment below. Use the beat’s explicit changes to pose or object state while retaining all other scene conditions. Do not illustrate future answers, repairs or visitor movements early.

[MScene] Scene CH03 06E Hold until the wheels stop
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L03 Elephant work rail and safe stand. Blue decorative elephant body on a wheeled base inside the physical-right work bay. Blocks and thick temporary support restrain it until the final strap is fitted and inspected. Low model stand and ribbon bench are on the safe side of a fixed work rail. Replacement wheels are supported in a rack on the working side; only helpers lift them. Ground stop is reachable by Tara from a clear fixed platform beside the near gallery stair. Costume trolley later parks on level paving beside the near work-bay edge; a short loading board slopes towards that bay but ends outside the blocked elephant wheels. Its brake and side handle remain reachable from level paving. The final strap follows its marked path; a length match alone does not establish structural safety.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Ahana at light trolley side handle, clown beside its brake, Tez on the sleeve corner. Navya and Vihaan at safe work stand. Tara returns by the near stair. Parade helper continues holding ground stop; broad helper at float.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. Float drive remains isolated. Trolley is separate from elephant. Temporary float strap and blocks remain until final support is fitted and inspected. All second-group guests stay behind their rope.
[MMeta] Planning. Show only the frozen moment below. Use the beat’s explicit changes to pose or object state while retaining all other scene conditions. Do not illustrate future answers, repairs or visitor movements early.

Blocks and thick temporary support restrain it until the final strap is fitted and inspected. Low model stand and ribbon bench are on the safe side of a fixed work rail. Replacement wheels are supported in a rack on the working side; only helpers lift them. Ground stop is reachable by Tara from a clear fixed platform beside the near gallery stair. Costume trolley later parks on level paving beside the near work-bay edge; a short loading board slopes towards that bay but ends outside the blocked elephant wheels. Its brake and side handle remain reachable from level paving. The final strap follows its marked path; a length match alone does not establish structural safety.

[MScene] Scene CH03 07A The theft they do not see
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L05 Counting arch and rear staff walkway. Far arch leads from the protected lane into Moon Square. Front reader and one silver slotted strip share a casing fixed at Navya’s waist height on the ground-level arch pier. Its rear cover opens into a recess hidden from the yard, accessible around the pier from safe paving. Upper rear walkway connects the bolted gallery door to a separate ticket-operated staff door. Existing near staff stair links walkway and recess: Veer descends to steal the strip, then returns upstairs before revealing it. This stair and the other approach serve Tara’s second pursuit without crossing the guest lane. Keep the same casing, slot and rear cover in every view. Writing shelf and message perch remain at the arch base.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Veer alone first on the upper rear walkway, then in the ground-level recess behind the counting-arch pier, reached by the existing near staff stair. Tara and children remain in the yard off-frame, attending to the float and clown.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. Reader-only cutaway. The first forty have already been counted. Second twenty-two have not yet crossed. The rear cover exposes the one installed strip. Veer’s ticket stays in his inner coat pocket; walking stick in his right hand until rested for two-handed work. No clown clothes are stolen in Chapter 03. The single casing stays fixed at Navya’s waist height above ground paving. Veer uses its rear cover from the same ground recess that Navya later reaches from the safe side.
[MMeta] Planning. Show only the frozen moment below. Use the beat’s explicit changes to pose or object state while retaining all other scene conditions. Do not illustrate future answers, repairs or visitor movements early.

[MScene] Scene CH03 07B A friend safe again
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L03 Elephant work rail and safe stand. Blue decorative elephant body on a wheeled base inside the physical-right work bay. Blocks and thick temporary support restrain it until the final strap is fitted and inspected. Low model stand and ribbon bench are on the safe side of a fixed work rail. Replacement wheels are supported in a rack on the working side; only helpers lift them. Ground stop is reachable by Tara from a clear fixed platform beside the near gallery stair. Costume trolley later parks on level paving beside the near work-bay edge; a short loading board slopes towards that bay but ends outside the blocked elephant wheels. Its brake and side handle remain reachable from level paving. The final strap follows its marked path; a length match alone does not establish structural safety.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Tara, trio, Tez and clown below at the secured work bay. Parade helper holds stop; broad helper checks final support. Guests watch from rope. Veer remains out of sight on rear walkway.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. Both float supports are secure. Ahana releases the light trolley only after its brake is checked and it is parked level between images by the clown. Tape stays at ribbon bench. Loose sleeve is clear of brake but not yet stowed. Counting-strip theft is unknown to everyone here.

[MScene] Scene CH03 07C Count the second group by hand
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L05 Counting arch and rear staff walkway. Far arch leads from the protected lane into Moon Square. Front reader and one silver slotted strip share a casing fixed at Navya’s waist height on the ground-level arch pier. Its rear cover opens into a recess hidden from the yard, accessible around the pier from safe paving. Upper rear walkway connects the bolted gallery door to a separate ticket-operated staff door. Existing near staff stair links walkway and recess: Veer descends to steal the strip, then returns upstairs before revealing it. This stair and the other approach serve Tara’s second pursuit without crossing the guest lane. Keep the same casing, slot and rear cover in every view. Writing shelf and message perch remain at the arch base.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Tara beside the arch’s safe-side writing shelf. Second group crosses with a plain escort helper. Navya inspects the automatic unit; Ahana, Vihaan and Tez wait behind Tara. Clown follows later with his trolley.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. First forty are already inside. The second group is exactly twenty-two, including Ira, Kabir and the girl in green with her friends. Trio are volunteers outside the 62 guest count. Tara uses one bound register and pencil; staff is supported beside the arch while she writes. Ahana has retrieved her lamp from the costume bench into her right hand before leaving; tape stays on the ribbon bench.
[MMeta] Planning. Show only the frozen moment below. Use the beat’s explicit changes to pose or object state while retaining all other scene conditions. Do not illustrate future answers, repairs or visitor movements early.

Strip remains in his coat until this reveal; ticket is in a different pocket. The empty casing stays downstairs. Guests continue to the square, clear of the loop.

Its rear cover opens into a recess hidden from the yard, accessible around the pier from safe paving. Upper rear walkway connects the bolted gallery door to a separate ticket-operated staff door. Existing near staff stair links walkway and recess: Veer descends to steal the strip, then returns upstairs before revealing it. This stair and the other approach serve Tara’s second pursuit without crossing the guest lane. Keep the same casing, slot and rear cover in every view. [MScene] Scene CH03 07F A bench blocks the path
[MMeta] DO NOT PRINT — Setting: Location CH03 L06 Moon Square board and fixed paving. From the Parade Arch: Clock Tower and Report Court left, Games Lane right, Grand Pavilion ahead. Pale paving surrounds low stage and three seating areas. Curved blue screen shelters one waiting group. Board and its accessible stop stand beside fixed outer paving, with a fixed shelf for the register. A folding bench can obstruct an inner path but never the preserved outer exit lane. First forty and next twenty-two make sixty-two guests; board stays at 40 after theft. Thirty are already seated, twelve by board, twenty behind screen. Ira and Kabir belong to twelve, girl in green and friends to twenty. No new signs displaying these subgroup answers yet.
[MMeta] Cast and positions. Tara reaches the board on the safe outer paving; Ira and Kabir near its path. Ahana beside them, Navya and Vihaan just behind. Tez stays on fixed paving. Other guests remain in their three waiting areas with seating helpers.
[MMeta] Props and continuity. Board display remains 40. Actual guest groups are 30 seated, 12 by board including Ira and Kabir, 20 behind blue screen including girl in green and her friends. Those counts are continuity data, not new visible labels or Chapter 04 solution. No actual child is missing.

[MMeta] Clarifications made while preserving the tasks
[MMeta] 1. The Acropolis/golden-ratio statement is presented as a claim to investigate rather than as established history. A documented example from Fondation Le Corbusier is supplied for the Internet-research task. Historical caution is supported by the mathematics outreach article “Myths of maths: The golden ratio”, Plus Maths, https://plus.maths.org/myths-maths-golden-ratio, accessed 19 October 2026. No historical construction intention is inferred from a photograph.
[MMeta] 2. Practice 3 A Question 6 is explained as a question about same-kind ratios. Comparisons between different kinds of quantities are not declared mathematically impossible; rates such as kilometres per litre retain their units. Likewise, the ratio notation 7:8 is distinguished from the fraction 7/8 without denying the relationship between them.
[MMeta] 3. Practice 3 B Question 7 retains SchoolB’s exact ratio 160:9. The answer key’s approximation of about 17 students per teacher is not used:160÷9 is about 17.78. The teaching compares 180:9 with 160:9 exactly. Whole-school ratios are not represented as actual class sizes.
[MMeta] 4. Fairness questions explicitly assume the same pay for equal time doing the same kind of work. Currency conversions use the rate stated in the question. The golden rectangle is an approximate ruler construction, clearly labelled; the completed drawing is not intended for measurement at printed size.
[MMeta] 5. The self-check preserves the book’s 19-point total and repeated mapping of Questions 4–6, with a short explanation. Revision 2 accepts any correct equivalent ratio, not only the sample answer or simplest form. All required source material was available; no question has been omitted because of a missing source.

[MMeta] Keep source question data separate from worked answers. Counting and drawing tasks remain unsolved until the answer heading; completed visual answers follow the attempt. All ratios, names, units, group counts, equal partitions and scale relationships must survive later layout changes. Native Word fractions, tables and prose remain editable; mathematical figure assets are embedded.
